Preface
The CompleteBook of
CONNECT4outlines
the strategic and tactical principles of
CONNECT
4 and allowsyou to assessyour skills with anyofseveral problem sets.
Please do not get discouraged ifthe problems seem quite difficult; even experts will
find many ofthem to be very difficult.
As you read this book, take a break from time to time and try one ofthe easy
or medium problem sets. The solutions at the back ofthe book show the winning
move, and some of them point you to a diagram and accompanying discussion
elsewhere in the book. As you reread the book, your play should improve, and
eventually you will want to try some ofthe harder problem sets.

How I Came to Write This Book Redtro play, with onlyone movetowin
CONNECT
I first started playing 4 at a
beach resort during the 1980s, where this
game was a popular way to pass the time
between swims. I took the game more seri-
ously than most ofthe other players, but even
after I figured out some basic principles about
odd-numbered rows and even-numbered
rows, there were still several opponents who

ing over and over (see Diagram 1), and I kept losing. I made it my goal to figure
out this opening.
Diagram 1 may still be a rather tricky problem even after you’ve read this
book, but perhaps you’d like to try the challenge ofsolving it right now! I give the

; ,

Although it’s been almost 25 years, I still remember saying “Aha! to myself
when I guessed Red’s winning move in Diagram 1. That was the moment I started
VII

to becomeagood player. I wanted toprovethat mymove in Diagram 1 was theonly
winning move, so I wrote a computer program to verify the solution. By analyzing
more than one billion positions, the computer program was able to confirm that
CONNECT
First Player can always win 4.
CONNECT
In 1988, 4 was the most difficult game that had ever been solved
by computer up to that time. It would have taken millions of dollars worth of
computer time to solve the game in 1970, but by 1988, processing speeds had
improved so that a thousand hours was enough time to solve the game on an
ordinary workstation. (Computers are even faster now, ofcourse, and your home
PC, properly programmed, could solve the game today in a matter ofminutes.)
Perfect computer play does not mean that it’s easy for a human to play per-
fectly. The game continued to fascinate me, and I continued to search for interest-
ing positions and principles ofwinning play. The results are in this book.
Acknowledgments
I want to thank Alan Pruce for much helpful criticism and input, especially the
'Truce Defense.’ Frank Stalone was also very helpful and introduced me to the
Third-Row Gambit. (Neither Alan nor Frank claims invention of these opening
styles.) John Tromp, a master programmer who has made important discover-
ies in go and other games, helped review the early draft but also deserves special
CONNECT
acknowledgment for another reason: While the 4 software I wrote
22 years ago to solve the game may have historic or sentimental interest, for several
years I’ve preferred to use John Tromp’s excellent Fhourstones implementation.
(Most of the analyses in this book have been verified by computer, but if there
are any errors, they were probably introduced during my editing and shouldn’t
be blamed on Fhourstones.) A special thank you goes to Jeffrey Harris who spent
many hours checking the logic in the discussions. (I hopeJeffrey has now become
CONNECT
an expert 4 player!) Finally, I should thank the anonymous bartender
in Bang Lamung, Thailand, who 25 years ago used Diagram 1 to show me that
CONNECT
4 is not an easy game!
viii

Introduction
CONNECT
4®, by Hasbro (and earlier, Milton Bradley), is a very popular game
among all age-groups and skill levels. The game is as simple to learn as tic-tac-toe
yet offers ample scope for tactics and strategy. Some people who seldom play other
CONNECT
games find 4 to be fun and to have just the right level ofchallenge.
Some gaming connoisseurs enjoy the several Web sites that offer online play with
CONNECT
4 experts. I can guarantee that ifyou studythis book, youwill be able
to thoroughly trounce most ofthe players who haven’t read the book.
—
CONNECT
4 is an alignment, or connection, game one strives to align, or
—
connect one’s pieces along a row, column, or diagonal but while other alignment
,
CONNECT
and connection games are played on horizontal boards, 4 is played
on a vertical board, with the disks dropping to the lowest empty slot in whichever
CONNECT
column they are played. As we will see, this makes the strategy of 4
vastly different from that ofother connection games. In most board games, you’d
be happy to make two moves in a row ifyour opponent offered to allow this. In
CONNECT
4, ifyour opponent makes this offer, it’s likely that he or she is trying
to trick you into exchanging a won game for a lost game!
The most famous connection game is the child’s game tic-tac-toe (also called
naughts and crosses or three-in-a-row), which was probably played at least as early
as Egypt’s Nineteenth Dynasty, 3,300 years ago, and certainly in ancient Rome
(perhaps under the name terni lapilli). A variety of other types of connection
games exist, including nine men’s morris and even bingo. The most difficult
among popular connection games may be the ancientJapanese game gomoku, but
CONNECT 4 is also extremely difficult. There are very few, ifany, human players
who can claim to have fully mastered it.
CONNECT
The basic concepts underlying 4 aren’t as old as tic-tac-toe or
gomoku, but they are old enough that the place and date ofits original invention
have been forgotten. Similar games have been played in many different countries
under many different names, including force four, four across, four-in-a-line, four-
in-a-row, line4, plot four, quadrow, stack4, tetralink, and the captain’s mistress. Its
foreign-language names include conecta 4 (Spanish), vier op ’n rij (Dutch), forza
quattro (Italian), puissance quatre (French) and Vier gewinnt (German). In this
CONNECT
book I discuss Hasbro ’s version of the game, named 4, previously a
registered trademark ofMilton Bradley.
IX

Captain James Cook
CONNECT
Most ofthe different names for 4 are self-explanatory, but the name
“captain’s mistress’’ has a special story. The captain in question was the famous
British explorerJames Cook (1728—79).
Captain James Cook was born to the family of a simple farm laborer but
becameanofficerintheBritishnavyandcaptainedthreeveryimportantexploration
voyages. He was the first European to discover several islands, including the
Hawaiian (or Sandwich) Islands, New Caledonia, Niue, and, ofcourse, the Cook
Islands. He searched (unsuccessfully) for the Northwest Passage from both the
Pacific and the Atlantic, and mapped Newfoundland, New Zealand, and many
points in between; he is most famous for being the first European to set foot on
Australia, claiming that sixth continent for the British Empire. (Cook failed in an
attempt to also be first to set foot on the seventh continent, Antarctica, though he
was first to cross the Antarctic Circle.)
Cook's first ship was His Majesty’s Bark Endeavour and his first mission was
,
to verify rumors ofa great continent west ofNew Zealand. As a “cover story” for
Cook’s secret mission to claim Australia for King George III, he first visited Tahiti
to observe the 1769 transit ofVenus; for this reason he was accompanied by the
astronomer Charles Green. Also on board Cook’s ship were two naturalists, Sir
Joseph Banks and Daniel Solander. (Australia’s Botany Bay is named after their
discoveries.)
During the long, uneventful days at sea, it is said that Cook and his scientist
friends entertained themselves playingavertical checkergame thatwasverysimilar
to whatwe now call CONNECT 4. Because ofCook’s fascination with this game,
the crew ofthe Endeavour (and his later ship HMS Resolution) called his wooden
board the captain’s mistress. Cook’s voyages were before the era ofsteam engines,
and vessels often listed sharply for long periods, even when the seas weren’t causing
yawing or rolling. We don't know whether Cook, Green, Banks, and Solander
played other board games, such as chess, but I suspect they chose their vertical
checker game because the pieces wouldn’t be knocked over by the ship’s listing or
CONNECT
rocking, while chess pieces might. (Today, 4 is a preferred game for
travel in cars and airplanes for the same reason.)
Before he made his famous discovery voyages, James Cook wrote that he
intended to go “not only farther than any man has been before me, but as far as I
CONNECT
think it is possible for a man to go.' Ifanyone tries to tell you that 4
is a pointless child’s game, remind them that a similar game (with the nickname
“captain’s mistress ”) was the pastime ofthe great Captain Cook.

Chapter
1
Rules and Terminology
CONNECT
I present the rules of 4 by showing a game as it unfolds. This also
gives us a chance to agree on some basic terminology.
CONNECT
The game of 4 is played by two players on a vertical board of
six rows and seven columns. One player has 21 disks of one color, say, red. The
other player has 21 disks ofanother color, say, black. It doesn’t matterwhat the two
CONNECT
colors are, as long as they can be easily distinguished. In 4, the name
ofthe game is the same as the object ofthe game. You want to align (connect) four
disks ofyour own color in a straight line. (Call thatfour-in-row.) The four disks
must be contiguous but may line up horizontally, vertically, or diagonally. As soon
—
as you’ve achieved this, the game is over you’ve won!
It may happen that even after both players play all 21 oftheir disks, neither
player has achieved four in row. The final position ofsuch a game is called a draw,
positions that will lead to draws assuming perfect future play may also be referred
to as draws, and the games leading to those positions may be called drawn games.
CONNECT
There is surprisingvariation in 4, dependingon a player’s style. Some
players may find 20 percent oftheir games ending in draws. Otherswill find drawn
games to be rare.

As the game continues in Diagram 4, Red threatens to achieve four-in-row
and win the game. While Red played in the cell Al, the empty cell B1 is the
important point. I will use threat to refer to any empty cell a player hopes to
occupy in the future. By analogy with chess, Red might say “check” as a courtesy
when he or she plays 7 Al (warning the Black player, since it isn’t much fun to
win through the opponent’s simple oversight). In this book, I use the term check
to describe a cell available for immediate play whose occupation would complete
four-in-row for one ofthe players, like B1 in Diagram 4. I’ve marked the check
with a red C (for “check”). (By the same analogy with chess, completion of a
game-winning four-in-row can be called mate.)

Black’s threat at b4 is shown with an uppercase T and called a major threat
because Black already has three of the four disks needed for four-in-row. Black

Red hasn’t achieved a major threat yet, but I use lowercase red t’s to show

for Black at e2-f2 since capturing both those cells would give Black a win. While
a cell that is available for immediate play is never called a major threat (it would be
called a check), it may be termed a minor threat, as e2 is here.
A
threat is called an odd-row threator an even-row threatifit is on, respectively,
an odd row (either the third or fifth row) or even row (either the second, fourth,
or sixth row); this attribute is called the threat’s rowparity. Remember that it is the
empty cell needed to complete four-in-row that determines the threat’s row parity.
Thetwo threatcells ofaminor threatpairmayhave thesame rowparityorthey may
have different row parities; in the latter case they are called a mixedthreatpair.

Terminology
During this first example game, I introduced some special terminology, such as
odd-row threat and major threat. We will need such words in order to explain
CONNECT 4 strategic principles. A useful term I neveruse in this book is double
threat. I avoid it because different people might interpret double threat in any of
several different ways:
A
• double check
• An adjacent threat pair
• A pair ofnon-adjacent same-colored threats in the same column
• A pair ofthreats by both Red and Black at the same cell
• A pair ofmajor threats
• A minor threat pair
Terms such as triplethreat, quadruplethreat, andsextuplethreatseem less likely
to cause confusion than double threat, and I do use these terms, giving them very
specific meanings.
How to Become a Master Player
This book shows you some of the tactical and strategic ideas you will need to
CONNECT
become a master at 4, but there is no secret formula for success.
CONNECT
4 is an easier game than chess or even checkers, but you’ll still need
to think for yourselfand plan ahead. Look back at Diagram 1; did you guess the
right move? Let’s deduce it step by step.
5

1. Red does have threats in the upper left side ofthe board, but they are
not quite enough to win, so Red must be prepared to force victory on
the right side depending on how Black defends.
2 . To have any chance ofbuilding a threat on the right side, Red must
somehow get past the Black major threat at F2.
3 . Ifyou’ve just started this book, you may not yet realize that a major
threat at F2 (or at any point on the second, fourth, or sixth row) is
not going to disappear by itself. Black will not play fl unless there is a
very strong compulsion to do so.
4 . Red can compel Black to play fl . All Red has to do is play C4. It may
not be obvious that Red will get C4, but he or she does get help from
his or her threat at C5. Anyway, Red can reason, “Getting C4 is my
only chance, so I may as well assume I will get it."
5 Now, when Black puts him- or herselfin Red’s shoes, Black will
.
realize that C4 is Red’s paramount goal. Can Black prevent that?
6
.
Yes; ifBlack plays b6, Red C4 loses immediately. Black wants to play
b6, but it’s Red’s turn.
7 . Red’s best move is Black’s best move. Red must play B6 so Black
doesn’t get it. Later Red will play C4, followed by F2. It may not be
clear yet exactly how Red will win in the upper right side, but ifRed
doesn’t play B6 right now, Black will play there and Red will have no
chance whatsoever on the right side.
—
This bookcontains manyexamplegameswith manyexpert moves too many
expert moves to explain each one in as much detail as I just did for Red’s disk 17 in
Diagram 1. Ifyou don’t understand the moves in a game, set them up on your own
board and try to figure them out. (Or, for more rapid experimentation, use one
CONNECT
ofthe fine 4 software packages, such as John Tromp’s Fhourstones.)
Most ofthe example games are played expertly, perhaps with one or two mistakes
to help make a point. (The game just reviewed in Diagrams 2-7 had many simple
mistakes just to introduce terminology.)
Before continuing. I’ll try to whet your appetite with another expertly-played

Chapter 2
Tactical Ideas
While strategy refers to the evaluation and selection of long-term threats, tactics
refer to the parries and thrusts that occur move by move. Obviously, strategy and
tactics work together; it is through tactical skill that a player is able to establish
winning strategic threats.
Value of Opponent’s Reply
An importanttactic is to makeyourdiskmore DIAGRAM 10
valuable than your opponent’s reply. In Dia-
Redtoplay,withonlyonemovetowin
gram 10, any ofthe central cells C5, D4, or
E3 might seem like a useful location for Red’s
disk 13, but C5 is the winning move. Black
can answer 13 D4 or E3 by taking a valuable
central cell (d5 or e4), but Red needn’t worry
about Black taking the remote cell c6. (But
judge all cases on their own merits; with a
slight change, D4 or E3 might be the win- Moves: D1! d2 D3! cl C2c3C4
ning move.) gl G2! g3 El! e2(13)_
Even betterthan amovethatgives up nostrongcell fortheopponent is one that
forces the opponent to waste a move. This idea is seen in several ofthe problems,
such as Problem 155, in which Black, already occupying f3 and f4, is delighted to
play 12 f5 since Red then has to waste a turn with the otherwise almost useless
blocking move at F6.

Preventing a Tactical Ploy
Ifyour opponent has a successful tactical ploy, it may be that you should have seen
it coming and prevented it. Often the ordinary strong-looking move will serve
this purpose, but sometimes you will have to make an unusual move to stop a
ploy. Diagram 17 shows an interesting example. Where should Red play to win in
Diagram 17? (These opening moves may look quite peculiar, but this is actuallyan
expertly played opening.)
I’ve indicated a possible future diagonal four-in-row that may lead to Red’s
victory; the threats at A5 and C3 are strong for Red because they are on odd
rows. With practice you will eventually be able to recognize a pattern like this
quickly. Red’s plan will be to play D2 when
DIAGRAM 17
appropriate to complete the minor odd-row Redtoplay,withonlyonemovetowin
threats at A5 and C3, but he or she must
6
watch out lest Black foil this with a push-
5
up play. An expert Black will play 10 a3 A4
4
a5 in response to 9 B5, or play as shown in
3
Diagram 18 in response to 9 E2. Thus, Red’s
2
winning play is 9 A3 to prevent this push-up
1
play. With the center still largely unplayed,
A3 will strike most players as an unlikely best
move. (Some Red players might try 9 D2 d3
Moves: D1! el Bl al B2! b3 B4a2(9)
first and then play 11 A3, but this allows Black 10 d3, 12 e2, followed by 14 d4
or e4.)
In Diagram 18, after Red’s mistaken 9 E2, most ofthe moves are shown with
exclamation marks; Black must play very precisely to get a draw, and Red must
defendvery precisely to ensure that Blackgets no better than a draw. Ifinstead Red
11

plays correctly with 9 A3, the game is likely to continue as shown in Diagram 19.
One way to understand whyA3 is a better play than E2 is to look at the cells above
these two moves, where Black would then have the opportunity to play. A4 is an
almost worthless cell, while E3 is a cell in a prime central location, which Black
would surely occupy ifRed gave him or her the chance.

More r Exampl.es of Quick, lV(i.ct4ory
It's always more fun to win quicklythan slowly, and sometimes a tactical sequencewill
lead to four-in-rowin justafewmoves. Often aplayerforcesanadjacent threatpairand
converts it to quickvictory. This applies in Diagram 21. Do you see Red’s quickwin?
Red’s winning move is shown in Diagram 22. With 23 E2, Red gets an
adjacent threat pair and wins quickly. In fact, 23 E2 is the only move for Red that
wins at all! Ifinstead Red plays as shown in Diagram 23, Black builds a major odd-
row threat and gets a draw.

the correct move, by visualizing where the key threats are or will come.

Chapter 3
Strategic Threats (Endgame Victories)
Mostgames between goodplayers arewon in theending, ratherthanwith aquickly
forced four-in-row early in the game. When a player does make a key sequence of
forcing moves early in the game, often he or she will do so to establish a long-term
strategic threat, rather than an immediate four-in-row. Therefore, a player must
understand which strategic threats will lead to a successful endgame, and which
threats are worthless.
Let us begin our study of strategicprinciples. Not only will these principles
tell you what threats of your own to seek, but they also will tell which of your
opponent’s potential threats to block. I first consider the simplest cases, and then
gradually study the effects ofcomplications.
Black Wins on the Second (or Fourth or Sixth) Row
If Red lets Black occupy two center cells in the second row (along with a third
cell underneath one ofthem), the game may be essentially over already, although
Black won’t complete four-in-row until the
DIAGRAM 30
ending. Diagram 30 shows an example of Blacktoplayandwin
this situation.
In Diagram 30, the game seems to have
just begun, and it is Red who may appear to
be buildingstrength in the uppercenterofthe
board. Yet Black is just as certain ofvictory
here as he or she was with the “fireworks” in
games like the one shown in Diagram 13.
Black has the entire second row locked
Moves: D1! d2 D3! el D4al
up with minor threats and will win easily as A2??e2! D5d6! E3(12)
long he or she avoids playing in thefirst row.
Red will eventually be forced to play in each remaining first-row cell, and Black
will get to play disks in all the second-row cells. If Black plays bl, cl, fl, or gl,
Black loses, but otherwise it hardly matters where Black plays; the simplest play is
for Blackto take e4 nowand thenjust playwherever Red plays. Thiswill eventually
lead to a position similar to the one in Diagram 31.

and you’ll see what I mean. A B C D E F G
The easiest way to prove that Black wins
Moves: D1 el E2?e3! D2d3! D4
in Diagram 32 is for Black to start by playing d5E4?d6! A1 cl! C2(14)__
14 a2. This leaves every column with an even
number of empty cells, and Black can then adopt the simple rule “Black plays
where Red just played.” Red ends up with every remaining cell on the first, third,
and fifth rows (and gets no four-in-row); Black ends up with every remaining
cell on the second, fourth, and sixth rows and completes not just four-in-row, but
seven-in-row on the sixth row.

As implied by the exclamation point, Black’s 12 cl in Diagram 32 was

First- and Second-Player Strategies Are Not the Same
CONNECT
ThestrategiesforRedand Blackaredifferentin 4. Black (Second Player)
will usually be very happy ifRed (First Player) makes the error oftrying for two Red
disks on an even-numbered row. Red should aim for threats on the third or fifth row.
(In the middle of a game, to know whether to strive for odd- or even-row
threats, you will often need to remind yourselfwhether you were First Player or
Second Player. Of course, it is usually very simple to figure out which player went
first; First Player virtually always plays his or her first disk at Dl.)
Zugzwang : It Is Not Always an Advantage to Move
I must introduce the concept of zugzwang,
DIAGRAM 34
which is essential to the strategic analysis of Whoeverplaysnextwill lose
CONNECT
endgames in 4. This German
word means “compulsion to move.”
In Diagram 34, C2 is a major threat for
both Red and Black; likewise, F2 is a major
threat for both players. Whoever moves next
will lose; the opposing player will get four-in-
row immediately afterward.
A B C D E F G
Zugzwang is a chess term referring to a
— Moves: Dl d2 D3! el D4e2G1??al??D5!
position where any move loses your only d6 E3??g2 ! ! G3g4G5g6A2a3A4a5A6
chancewould be to passyour move, but that’s e4! E5e6! B1 b2! B3b4! B5b6! (31)__
89

not allowed. Diagram 35 gives another, less obvious, example ofzugzwang. Ifit is
Red’s move, then Black wins; ifit is Black’s move, then Red wins!
That the position shown in Diagram 35 is zugzwang is far from obvious;
there’s still a lot ofplay left in the game, and both players will need to be careful.

whoever plays next loses. Suppose it’s Black’s turn and he or she temporizes by
putting a disk at e6. Where must Red then play to win?
I hope you saw that Red must make the temporizing move at Cl, forcing
Black to play a3. IfRed instead plays A3, then Black plays a4 and wins with the
adjacent threat pair at C2-C3.
If it were Red’s turn to play in Diagram 36, Red would be the one forced
to play A3; Black would play a4 and win with the adjacent threat pair. Ifyou’ve
played much CONNECT 4, you already realize that zugzwang is a very common
occurrence (although you may never have heard this unusual German word). In
cases such as Diagram 35, where one player is in zugzwang but his or her moves do
not quickly lead to a loss, I often say that the other player has zugzwangcontrol.
Even-Row Threats Are Not Influential
In Diagram 37, Red wins with a major odd-row threat, even though Black has two
columns with major even-row threats.
Black can play f2 and lose at once, or play b3 (or e3) and sacrifice one ofhis
or her even-row threats. Sacrificing an even-row threat will do Black no good;
after b3 B4 b5 B6, it will be Black’s turn to play again, and he or she will be in
the same dilemma. In general, when all the safe cells have been filled in a column

93

In the remainder ofthis chapter, I explain these general principles and present
some transformation and victory rules. The transformation rules explain which
threats can be ignored andwhich can be simplified; after I’ve simplified the threats
using these transformations, the victory rules explain who will win the game.
The rules in this chapter are notcomplete, because they do not cover the fol-
lowing points:
• Complicated configurations ofminor threats
• Columns with three or more major threats
• Tactical interactions between columns
Nevertheless, the principles will guide you to understand which potential
threats have value and which don’t, and if you understand these “simple” cases,
you should be able to figure out more complicated cases as well.
General Principles
Gl: In the final column, Black gets the
even-row cells, and Red gets the odd-row cells.
Ifthe board ends up completely filled in, Black will place the final disk (disk
42) on a sixth-row cell. Working backward from there, Principle Gl becomes
obvious. This principle implies that Red should seek odd-row threats, while Black
should seek even-row threats.
G2: Red’s odd-row threat has priority over a
Black even-row threat in a separate column.
In the diagram, ifBlack moves in the first column, Red wins at once. It does
no good for Black to play in the second column, because that column has an even
number ofvacant cells, and Black will be in zugzwang anyway after those vacant
cells are taken. However, ifit is Red’s move in the diagram, he or she can play in
the first column, forfeiting his or her own threat, but there are
an oddnumber ofvacant cells in that column and thus it will be
Black’s turn to move (and forfeit his or her own threat) when the
second column is finally played.
In other words, even-row threats have no effect on zugzwang
control, but odd-row threats lead to a reversal ofzugzwangcontrol.
I refer to columns with an odd-row threat as odd-threatedcolumns.
94

G3: Red (First Player) likes odd numbers.
With no odd-threated columns, Blackwins ifhe orshe has an even-rowthreat,
because then Red will be in zugzwang in the endgame. With one odd-threated
column, Red wins ifthe odd-row threat is Red’s; Red has zugzwang control. With
a second odd-threated column belonging to Black, zugzwang control reverses back
to Black, and so on. So Red (who can win only in an oddrow) wants there to be an
oddnumber ofodd-threated columns.
Recall this principle by memorizing the mantra: “Red (First Player) likes odd
numbers.’’

G4: Adjacent major threats win.
IfBlack has major threats at two adjacent cells, such as b4 and b5, he or she
can simply play up their column and win, without waiting for the endgame.
Sometimes one playerwill have adjacent threats on one side ofthe boardwhile
the other playerhas adjacent threats on the otherside. This leads to a race; whoever
plays up to his or her threats first wins.
G5: Second-row and fifth-row threats work together.
When the two threats at these two points are of two different colors, the
second-row threat has maximum value; the fifth-row threat is undercut and so is
worthless.
When the two threats are both one player’s, he or she can force the opposing
player into zugzwang, later forfeit the second-row threat, and use the fifth-row
threat to force the opposing player into zugzwang a second time. Perhaps a simple
way to think ofthis is to memorize that a same-coloredsecond- andfifth-row threat
pairis equivalent to two odd-threatedcolumns.

Chapter 5
Undercutting Threats
IfRed prepares a major odd-row threat on the right side, say, at F5, Black has two
ways to respond. (1) Black can form his or her own odd-row threat on the left
—
side, perhaps at a3 or a5, getting a draw this is called counterthreat. (2) Black
can undercut Red’s F5 threat with his or her own major threat at f2 or f4. IfBlack
is sure that Red won’t get a second major odd-row threat, he or she will naturally
prefer awinning undercutting threat to a counterthreat good enough only to draw.
IfRed does get two major odd-row threats, Black will usually seek a counterthreat
andan undercutting threat.
If Red has a threat at C5, Black can undercut that threat by constructing a
threat at c4. More generally, any threat is an undercuttingthreatifit lies in the same
column and immediately underneath a threat of the opposing player. Unlike an
ordinary Black threat at c4, whose existence would make Black reluctant to play
c3, an adjacent-row undercutting threat can be played up to; Blackwould be happy
to play c3 ifc3 C4 c5 wins. A first-row check that destroys a second-row threat is
also termed undercutting.
As aspecial case, asecond-rowthreat undercuts an opponent’s fifth-row threat,
though it isn’t on an adjacent row.
An undercutting threat usually renders the threat above it almost worthless.
The undercutting threat need not be a major threat; indeed, interesting examples
usually involve two minor threats along a diagonal. Sometimes one disk (three
minor-threat fulfillments needed for four-in-row) is enough for a successful
undercut.

Chapter 6
Counterthreats
When Red acquires a major odd-row threat on one side of the board that Black
is unable to undercut, Black must try to build his or her own threat on the other
side ofthe board. IfBlack builds no such threats, or only even-row threats, Black
loses. IfBlack builds one or two odd-row threats in columns separate from Red’s
threat, Black draws. IfBlack builds two odd-rowthreats, including one in the same
column as Red’s threat, Black wins. Black also wins with odd-row threats in any
three columns, but this is rarely achieved. The most common way for Black to win
when Red has a major odd-row threat is for Black to construct an odd-row threat
andan even-row threat.
A Black odd-row threat that neutralizes a Red odd-row threat is called a
counterthreat. Black tries to form a counterthreat in many ofour example games.
For the example in this chapter, let’s look at an exciting game where Black’s even-
and odd-row threats are adjacent, and it might appear he or she cannot get both
and will have to choose one or the other. But Black plays carefully and gets the
cake (his or her odd-row threat) and eats it too (wins with an even-row threat).
This game teaches an important lesson: Sometimes a player needs to delay the
completion ofa threat.

113

Chapter 7
Minor Threats
So far I have dealt mostly with major threats (cells whose capture would complete
a four-in-row), but minor threats (cells whose capture leads to a major threat) are
also important. And ofcourse, every major threat starts life as a minor threat.
I can’t provide detailed transformation rules for minor threats as I did for
major threats; there are too many cases, and sometimes six or more minor threats
interact to create a win. Instead I explore minor threats by looking at examples.
There are a few basic patterns you will see over and over.
One can always hope that a minor threat will become a major threat, perhaps
due to one’s opponent’s error, but I usually do not speak of minor threats unless
they already have strategic significance. I divide such strategic threats into six
categories:
• Simple Black even-row threat.
• Mixed diagonal undercutting threat. A mixed threat pair (even row
and odd row) that has no offensive value may nonetheless undercut
an odd-row threat above it.
• Triple threat. Three (or four) threats sometimes work together to
have the same strategic value as one major odd-row threat. Such
configurations are so common and important that I give them the
name
triple threat.
• Winning quadruple threat. This is a specific but common
configuration offour threats that win.
• Sextuple threat. This is a common configuration ofsix threats that are
equivalent to a triple threat.
• Plethora ofthreats. Sometimes five or more threats work together to
create or counter a winning threat. Such situations may arise when
one player has a preponderance ofdisks in the upper center ofthe
board.
I have already discussed Black minor even-row threats. I mention them here
just for completeness. These threats are easy to understand, but they are important
since they lead directly to Black victory whenever Red cannot form a winning
115

,
odd-row threat. I will now clarify the other five categories ofstrategic minor threat
with examples.

Chapter 8
Review of Strategy and Tactics
Earlier chapters covered the basic ideas ofwinning play. In this chapter I give fur-
ther examples illustrating those ideas.

G to establish a triple threat on the right-hand side, and yet still loses to Black’s
powerful threats on the left side. Assuming that Red’s right-side configuration is
equivalent to a major odd-row threat, on the left side Black will need an odd-row
threat (to cancel Red’s odd-row threat) and an even-rowthreat (to get awin instead
ofjust a draw). Black has an even-row threat (at b4) already (unless Red establishes
a B3 threat to undercut it); the question is: Will Black get an odd-row threat on
the left side?
I’ve indicated several Black and Red threats in Diagram 118 to show how
the analysis proceeds. The triple threat at a5-b5-b4 would provide Black with the
equivalent ofa major odd-row threat, but Red’s diagonal threats at A4-B3 would
normally undercut Black’s a5 threat and thus thwart Black. (When the A column
is played, Black will want a4 so Red doesn’t get the B3 major threat, but Red
then takes A5 to kill Black’s odd-row threat.) However, Black can undercut Red’s
137

diagonal by playing al, and if Red plays Al, Black plays a2 to build a major odd-
row threat at b3. (I’ve tried to depict this last possibility by showing b3 as Black’s
threat cell.)
In other words, Black might wish to play al -and construct a threat at b2,
not to build an even-row threat in column B (Black already has one at b4), but to
neutralize any possible Red threat at B3.
Did you guess where Red’s blunder was in Diagram 118? The cell D5 is one
ofthe most powerful on the board; here it leads to Black’s fifth-row counterthreat
and therefore victory. It was essential for Red to take D5 at the first possible
chance, answering 4 d4 with 3 D5. (As the game played out, Black had to play
almost exactly as he or she did to win and never got a chance to take d5 until
disk 18. However, once Red passed up the first chance, Red never got an effective
opportunity to play D5 either.)
I asked you to guess Black’s next move in Diagram 118. Black has several
winning moves (though not 20 f2 G3, with Red getting double check). Did you
guess 20 e6 to build an adjacent threat pair at b3-b4? Sorry, that doesn’t work. In
Diagram 119, you will see that Red would then get an adjacent threat pair and win
the resultant race.

Overcutting Threats
A major threat can neutralize the opponent’s minor threat underneath it. Whereas
an undercutting threat can neutralize both major and minor threats, an overcutting
threat lies above the opponent’s threat and will have little value unless the oppo-
nent’s threat is just a minor threat. (I write DIAGRAM 129
“little value’’ rather than “no value” because Blacktoplay,withonlyonemovetowin
a blocking disk must be spent when the col-
umn is played, and sometimes the waste ofa
move is critical.)
Here’saratherdifficultproblem inwhich
a key theme is an overcutting threat. Where
should Black play in Diagram 129?
Diagram 129,which islikelytoariseafter
a very common opening, is a complicated
Moves: D1! d2 D3! d4 D5! el E2 e3 E4!
position, and it may seem far too early to bl B2b3 B4b5 D6gl E5??(18)__
predict the outcome just by examining the
strategic threats. Nevertheless, we can understand how Red intends to win using
the threats I’ve indicated in the diagram. Before discussing those Red threats, we
note that Black has two ways to build a major threat in Diagram 129: The move
18 cl would build a threat at f4, and 18 b6 would build a threat at c5. But just one
ofthose moves wins; the other loses.
If C4 were a Red major threat, Red would have a routine win with a triple
threat A3-C5-C4. But of course Red can’t play FI to establish C4 as a major
threat; Red’s minor threat at Fl is overcutby Black’s major threat at f2.
But Red’s C4 is still a valid threat. Red will play up column C eventually
and take C4. (Black can’t take c4 because Red C5 turns A3 into a majorodd-row
threat.) As soon as Red takes C4, Black must answer at fl, and Black’s f2 threat
then disappears. After this, Red’s right-side triple threat F5-G5-G6 becomes active
and gives Red victory.
But Black can foil this plan! Return to Diagram 129, and figure out Black’s
winning move. Diagram 130 shows the result ofBlack’s play 18 b6; Diagram 131
shows the result ofBlack’s play 18 cl.
Diagram 130 shows the solution to Diagram 129. Black’s 18 b6 constructs a
threat at c5 and thus overcuts Red’s threat to playc4. As long has Black never plays
in anyofthe cells marked “no” (and plays above Red when Red plays in such a cell)
Black will win easily with the major even-row threat at f2. (The same key move
also occurs in Diagram 84.)

“I Play Where You Play”
Surprisingly often, the best play for a one player will be to play above the opposing
player’s just-played disk; the Candlesticks Opening, in Diagram 236 (see Chapter
12), is one example.
When you can identify a situation where this imitative play is good enough,
it makes successful play very easy. There are some proofs about openings based
on imitative play. For example, Black never loses against opening Red 1 Bl. In
Diagram 133, the bicolored disks may be either red or black, but they do alternate
in color up the columns. That is, Black takes either c2 or c3, and so on. (Similarly,
Black waits for Red to take Fl or Gl, then takes the other.) You will see that if
142

Red gets four-in-row on, say, the third row, DIAGRAM 133
Black must have achieved four-in-row earlier
Redtoplay, doesnotwin
on the second row. Victor Allis was the first
to prove, in this simple way, that Black can
be assured of at least a draw after Red 1 B1
or Red 1 Cl.
Similarly, Black can be assured ofvictory
if Red starts with 1 Al. Black does this by
answering Al at dl; playing in column D
(and then “switching off the autopilot ”) as
soon as Red plays at A4, B5, C3, E3, F5, or Gl; making a winning move (in
column F or B) after D6; and otherwise playing immediatelyabove Red’s last disk.
Red’s only counter to this Black strategy is to play up column D to prevent Black
from forminganyhorizontal even-row threat.

Chapter 10
Sample Games
In this chapter I review a few example games in detail. For some games, I discuss
several variations; keep in mind that the section title (for example, “Triple Threat
Versus Triple Threat,”) might not apply to all of the variations. (Many of these
games are based on standard openings, reviewed in later chapters, such as the 5-4
Opening and the 3-4 Opening.)
Triple Threat Versus Triple Threat
I begin by reviewing games related to the triple threat in Diagram 137 (also shown
as Diagram 65 in Chapter 7).

By trying to show the winning threat configuration that Red intends to
achieve, I may have misled the reader into thinking that Diagram 139 is a cut-and-
dried position with no interesting playleft. This is far from the case. Black may get
a counterthreat on the left side, and there’s another Black threat that might cause
Red to waste a keyturn. Suppose Black plays 12 gl in Diagram 139; can you figure
out where Red must then play?
Red must answer 12 gl with 13 G2. Otherwise, Diagram 140 or 141 might
result. In Diagram 140, Red has a plethora ofthreats that, though notgood enough
to win, salvage a draw in the face of Black’s major threat at d4. In Diagram 141,
Red makes a second blunder (15 E5), after which Black 16 d2 threatens to play d3
and a2, but Red can block only one ofthose.

I’m sure the reader knew that Black had to play 28 fl in Diagram 142, since
alternatives sacrifice Black’s threats. But since column F has an even number of
empty cells in Diagram 142, it might seem that play there just postpones the

the play in column F is essential because it is Red who suffers zugzwang and who
must let Black capture Red’s threat cell F5. Once Black accomplishes this, Black
no longer needs an odd-row threat and is happy to play a2.

To achieve a draw in the face ofBlack’s major even-row threats, it is essential
that Red threaten a winning counterattack. Red’s major threats at B5 and F5 are
worthless since they’re undercut. Some possible future minor odd-row diagonal
threats for Red are marked in Diagram 157, but it will take a lot ofwork for Red
to develop these. In particular, D5 is essential, but because ofa variation, shown in
Diagram 158, Red may be unable to afford to take D5 even when Black plays d4.
To prevent Black from getting D5 or the double check shown in Diagram
158, Red must play on the C5-F2-G1 diagonal, but not at F2, which lets Blackwin
easily. The choices for Red in Diagram 157 are therefore 15 C5 or 15 Gl. Before
considering either move, note that (ifBlack is a perfect player) Red can’t hope for
better than a draw, since if Black is worried about the counterattack, Black can
force a draw with ease, as shown in Diagram 159, for example.

Out of Nowhere
The game in Diagram 165 seems to contradict our basic principles. Red has allowed
Black to get two disks on the third row, while Red hasn’t a single disk there. Yet Red
has playedexcellentlyand shouldwin this game easily. In addition to future prospects,
Red threatens to playon the C1-F4 or G1-D4 diagonals and establish E3 as a threat.
In fact, ifBlack refuses to allow Red to establish the E3 threat, the game ends
quickly, as shown in Diagram 166 ...

Now Red has no useful threat; Black will win in the sixth row. Why did
this happen? Red’s diagonal threats B3-D5, though too feeble to win, are the
undercutting threat Red needs to prevent Black from winning. Red wants to force
Black to block at d5 eventually, then take D6 and kill Black’s sixth-row threat.
This gameplay is shown in Diagram 173.

Chapter
1
Rule Variations
CONNECT
You may start to lose interest in 4 playing by the standard rules if
the same positions keep recurring. Fortunately, there are other games that can be
played with the standard CONNECT 4 game set. A few ofthese alternate games
are described here.
Two Disks at a Time
CONNECT
The rules of this variant are the same as those for regular 4, except
that a player plays two disks at each turn instead ofone. The first player to get four-
in-row wins. (To avoid giving Red a big advantage, only one disk is played at the
very first turn.)
This game can always bewon by Black, but it isn’t easy, and Red will often win
in practice. Diagram 188 shows a game where Black plays very precisely to win; two
threats (d4-d5 and e2-e3) cannot both be stopped. (The two-disk games shown in
this section are notannotated with exclamation points and question marks.)

Red Handicap
Since First Player (Red) has the advantage, players can try make the game by
|           |          |     |      |       |             |     |     | to    |            |     | fair |

166

Herearesix possible Red handicaps. For the First three Redcan still forcevictory
with perfect play. For the last three the game will be drawn with perfect play.
• Any four-in-row involving A6 does not count as a win for Red (but
would count for Black). This is called BlueA6 because, ifyou have a
second set with disks ofa different color, say, blue, then you’ll have
—
Red use a blue disk when he plays at A6 a four-in-row using the
blue disk won’t even look like a four-in-row. Red can win despite this
handicap ifhe plays perfectly. In this variant, A6 is called a blue cell
for Red.
• A horizontal sixth-row four-in-row does not count as a win for Red
(but would count for Black). Diagram 192 shows a familiar opening
that no longer wins for Red ifhorizontal sixth-row four-in-rows don’t
win.
• Both A6 and G6 are blue cells.
• Any four-in-row using A2 does not count as a win for Red (but would
count for Black). (With this handicap, Black can draw after 1 D1 el.)
• Certain initial moves are required or forbidden (see the next section,
“Opening Lottery”).
• D6 is a blue cell. (Black draws with DdDdDcFg or DdDdDcCc [the
columns in the order they were played].)
Since Red just barely can force victory when Black plays perfectly, it may be
surprising that Red can still win whenA6 and G6 are blue, orwhen all Red's sixth-
row horizontal wins are excluded. In fact, I’ve verified with computer assistance
that Red can win with as many as nine cells treated as blue simultaneously! (Red
can force victory only when the nine blue cells are A6, B6, C6, E6, F6, G6, B4,
F4, and either A5 or G5.)
Opening Lottery
You and your partner may play the same openings over and over again, perhaps
DbB or DcG. One way to regain interest quickly is to switch openings. Do as they
do in checkers tournaments: Use dice, coins, or lottery to choose a forced open-
ing. In Chapter 9 you can find which openings lead, theoretically, to a draw or to
Red or Black victory, or you can fashion a set ofsix three-disk openings from the
following lists.

Once Black takes 14 b5 in Diagram 192 to block the fifth-row horizontal
threat, E5 loses much of its value; Gl and D6 become the important cells, and
Red will play disk 15 at one of them. If Red plays 15 Gl, Black should answer
with 16 d6, and vice versa. (These are the two most valuable cells, so it is logical
that each player gets one ofthem.) Diagram 192 shows the latter possibility. Red
already has two minor odd-row threats but needs a third threat to win. Black’s gl
has prevented Red FI (which would build the third threat at C4) and neutralized
Red G2 (a Red threat at F3 is undercut). In otherwords, it overcuts Red’s Fl threat
and undercuts Red’s F3 threat. Black’s gl accomplishes a third important purpose
as well: It builds a major even-row threat at f2.
Although Black’s gl was an excellent play, Red can still win. (Red always wins
ifhe or she makes no mistake, and Red has made no mistake yet.) Where should

D3-D4 Opening
I use the term D3-D4openingv/\\zn Red gets both these keycells earlyin the game.
This position often is very powerful for Red (although when Red already has C4
or E4, as shown in variations ofthe 3-4 opening, it’s often better for Red to wait
and try to get D5).
Diagram 230 indicates how easy it often is for Red to win when he or she
gets D3-D4 early. Red starts by building an attack in the lower right side; by the
time Black counters that, Red has overwhelming strength in the upper center of
the board.
Diagram 231 shows another variant of the D3-D4 opening. Where should
Red play next? I think even most experts will get this wrong.

1-4 Opening
After 1 D1 el, Red must play 3 A1 or 3 Bl, but many players don’t realize that.
If Red plays 3 D2, Black will answer with 4 d3 and have little trouble winning,
but the Red play 3 E2 presents more difficulty. Play will develop as shown in Dia-
gram 250. This opening is neither a central-column opening (only one early disk
is played in column D) nor a first-row opening (only two ofthe first five disks are
played in the first row); I call this the 1-4opening. (This is the only “standard open-
ing” I consider in which Red cannot force victory.) Black has only one move to

Chapter 13
First-Row Openings
When Red has mastered the central-column opening, Black will usually start
answering Red’s D1 with 2 el or fl. (Black 2 gl is too weak to consider.) Often
more disks are soon played in the first row, giving these variants a generic name,
first-row opening (defined arbitrarily as any game in which at least three ofthe first
four disks are played in the first row). I refer, briefly, to the DcF, DcG, DbF, DbB,
and DaG variants (others sometimes call these the “436,” “437,” “426,” “422,”
and “417” openings). First-row openings admit more variety than central-column
openings, and a line from one initial sequence may transpose into a line from a
different sequence.

Diagram 284 shows one of my favorite openings. Red probes on the second
rowwith B2 and C2, but Black answers at b3 and c3 to deny Red a presence on the
third row. Finally Red plays D2 forcing Black to block at A2; then Red is able to
seize D3. Red ends up getting only a single disk on the third row, but it’s enough
to win; Red has a sextuple threat.
Diagram 283 shows awell-played opening; Redwillwin, ifhe orshe continues
to be careful, because ofRed’s triple minor threat on the fifth row.
IfRed substitutes 7 C2 in this opening, Blackwill probably take c3, as shown
in Diagram 286.

IfBlack replaces 12 d2 with b3 in Diagram 293, Diagram 298 might follow. I

Finally, Red may overlook the urgency of3 Al in these openings and play 3
E2, as shown in Diagram 299. In that diagram, Red has a triple threat in the lower
right, but Black's b4-el diagonal undercuts it. (Red’s diagonal threats A4-C2 are
both even-row threats so are useless to Red, even for undercutting.) Observe the
exquisite precision with which Black plays to achieve a draw. Although Black’s
threat c3-d2 is key to his or her draw, Black must not play d2.
Third-Row Gambit
In chess, a gambit is a ploy in which one player sacrifices something hoping for
compensation. In the Third-Row Gambit Black lets Red establish an early major
,
third-row threat but hopes to get compensation, usually in the fifth row.
Diagram 300 shows a typical Third-Row Gambit. Note that Red has played
four disks to Black’s two on the right side (columns E, F, and G), while Black has
a similar two-disk advantage on the left side.
DIAGRAM 300
Furthermore, Red dominates the third row, Redtoplay,withonlyonemovetowin
while Black dominates the fifth row. This
lopsided disk distribution is typical of the
gambit opening. Black lets Red make an easy
third-rowthreaton the rightside butdevelops
a strong attack and is likely to get an odd-row
counterthreat on the left side. In Diagram
300, Red has six losing moves! What is Red's
only move to win? (Hint: Where does Black „
intend to build odd-row threats?) F3! d5F4! f5D6! bl B2! b3(17) _

(Note that by now, Red has a three-disk preponderance on the right side, and Black
a similar preponderance on the left side.) As implied by the depicted threats, Red
can play_E2 now to get a routine triple threat, though to win Red will still have
to play carefully and prevent a Black counterthreat on the left side. Note the value
ofE2 in this opening; ifyou focus your attention on just the third row, you may
overlook that E2 forms a triple threat. (As we will see presently, in some variations
ofthe Third-Row Gambit, D2 plays a role like E2 does here.)
Although Red can play E2 now and win, he or she may prefer to defer this
move and work to counter Black’s left-side attack. After all, Black can hardly play
— —
e2 it would give Red two major odd-row threats so there may seem to be no
rush for Red to play E2. But let’s look at Diagram 303, where mistiming E2 turns
out to be a mistake.

Red’s move 23 B5 was essential; otherwise, Black will obtain a triple threat
that overcomes Red’s triple threat, as shown in Diagram 306.
Black has several threats here; most simply, Black can play up column C to
get c5 and a winning pair of major odd-row threats. However, the triple threat
depicted in Diagram 306 is all Black needs for victory. Since three columns (A, C,
and E) have odd-row threats and three is an odd number, you might think that
Red has zugzwang control. But don't forget that a threat pair like Black’s c4-c5
should be counted as two columns when computing zugzwang control.

finish the game with an a2-b3-c4-d5 win.)

Black has achieved a major odd-row threat at c5. (If Red had played 29 A3
instead of B6, Black would take b6 with the same result.) Red will get a major
odd-row threat along the E5-G3 diagonal, of course, but odd-row threat versus
odd-row threat is a draw. Red can alwayswin by playingperfectly so where did Red
,
go wrong in this variation?
The answer is that it was Red’s piece at A4 that allowed Black to play a5 and
build a major threat. Red’s 23 A4 seemed to have (a tiny bit of) offensive and
defensive value, but it ended up helping Black.
If we look at the alternatives to 23 A4, we will see that almost all of them
help Black! Red’s only winning move in Diagram 319 is 23 G2, a move that has
absolutely no offensive or defensive significance! (See Diagram 321 for the result.)
The guiding principle here is simple: Black seeks fifth-row horizontal threats,
so both players should avoid the fourth row. IfRed plays the (surprising) winning

D5 is often a key cell, and Red may be tempted to take it here, but this leads
to a quick Black victory, as shown in Diagram 14 (see Chapter 2). Instead, Red
must play 13 B2 or 13 C2. The game might then continue as shown in Diagram
339, with 17 D6 being a subtle blunder. Where should Black play, after 17 D6, to
win?
In Diagram 340, Black plays perfectly at 16 al, 18 a2, 20 b3, and 22 b5 and
builds a left-side triple threat that, when combined with Black s major threat at e3,
gives Black an easy win. (You may think ofc4-c5 has an adjacent threat pair that,
when played out, produces a major odd-row threat for Black at a5.)

Black threatens b3 B4 b5 a push-up play that would establish a major odd-

the Black threat. Black had nothing better to offer. Diagram 349 shows another
variation with a similar result.

The DcF opening is, of course, the same as the DeB opening; so that you
can practice viewing positions both ways, I show most problems and some further
examples using this mirror-image form.
Diagram 356 shows a variation ofDiagram 354 where Black makes a peculiar
blunder. How can Red now salvage a draw? (There are two successful first moves.)
Diagram 357 shows one answer to Diagram 356. (Red can also start with 21
C5.)
The solution to this problem posed in Diagram 138 is as follows. If Black
plays as shown in Diagram 358, he or she will soon be in zugzwang and forced
to let Red get D5. IfBlack plays 24 d4, a series offorced moves follow, with Red
finally playing D6 with a winning threat at A3. Instead, Black must temporize
with 24 g6, as shown in Diagram 359.

C4d2! F4f5! D3d4! D5d6! (17) Red should play 7 D2 instead of F3 in
this opening but will need to continue skillfully. In Diagram 363, Red has solved
many problems correctly but isn’t out ofthe woods yet. Red has odd-row threats in
all four remaining columns; in other words, Red has a (nonstandard) triple threat
on the right side and another (sort of) triple threat on the left side. Black’s major
odd-row threat at e5 might lead to a drawn game against a triple threat involving
column E (Black would play up column E so that Red’s major threat ends up in
column G) but perhaps not against a left-side triple threat, let alone two. However,
the left-side triple threat is not properly formed (the threat B5 depends on E2,
which in turn relies on E3), so the outcome will depend on timing.

Diagram 369 is another interesting variant.
In Diagram 301, Red’s winning move is 21 A2, as shown in Diagram 370.
Red gets a triple threat on the right side and a major threat at C4 to undercut
Black’s potential threat at c5. Black’s 24 a4 may seem to be a weak move, since
Black’s chances evaporate when his or her minor threat at c3 evaporates, but Black
has no good alternative. IfBlack held a2 instead ofRed, Blackwould already have a
major threat at c4, forming a triple threat a5-c3-c4, and would even be threatening
the push-up play a3 A4 a5 to get a major threat at c3.
The “logical’’ move 21 E2 in Diagram 301 loses to e3, as shown in Diagram
371, which ends in an elegant flurry offorcing moves.

Chapter 14
CONNECT
4 PopOut
CONNECT
In 2009, Hasbro began marketing a new 4 set with a “PopOut bar.’’
With this set it is possible to poke a disk in the bottom row and pop it out, or
remove it from the board. When the disk pops out, any other disks in the same
column descend, under the force ofgravity. For example, ifyou pop out the disk at
Bl, the disk originally at B2 will move to Bl, the disk at B3 to B2, B4 to B3, and
so on.
Hasbro includes rules for three games in its new set:
—
CONNECT
• Original 4 the game I discuss in 12 ofthe First 13
chapters ofthis book.
—
CONNECT
• PopOut a game similar to original 4, but with a
special pop-out move.
—
• Pop 10 a fun, fast-moving game in which every move is a pop-out
move.
In each ofthe pop-out games (PopOut and Pop 10), you are allowed to pop
out disks only ofyour own color. There is a PopOut bar with two positions: open
CONNECT
for the pop-out games, closed for original 4. (It is easy to change
the PopOut bar position in the middle ofa game; when playing PopOut, heavy-
handed players might even prefer to leave the bar in the closed position except
when actually making a pop-out move.)
PopOut
PopOut is a fun and exciting game that many players will prefer to the original
CONNECT 4 game. Space constraints do not permit me to treat it in as much
CONNECT
depth as I have done for the original 4; here I give the rules, strategic
principles, and a few illustrative games.
Rules of PopOut
The rules of PopOut are exactly the same as those of CONNECT 4, except for
the pop-out move. At a player’s turn, insteadofdropping a disk ofhis or her color
into the board at the top, he or she may pop a disk ofhis or her own color out at
227

the bottom, with remaining disks in that column each descending one row. Ifthis
pop-out move leads to a four-in-row, the game is won by the player whose color
matches the four-in-row. There are three special rules to handle special cases associ-
ated with PopOut:
1. Ifthe pop-out move leads to two (or more) four-in-rows, so that both
Red and Black seem to have won, then theplayer who made thepop-
out move wins.
2 . When the board is completely full (with 42 disks), the player to move
has his or her choice: play a pop-out move or declare the game to be
drawn.
3 . It may happen that a repeating series ofpop-out moves and regular
moves means a game never ends. (This is similar to a “perpetual
check” in chess.) When this happens, either player can point out that
the same game position has occurred at least three times and declare
the game drawn.
While the first special rule will need to be applied quite frequently, it will be
more seldom that the second and third special rules come into play. You might
even guess that the repeating moves in the third special rule would happen only
ifboth players were being deliberately silly, but the game in Diagram 404 (see the
section “PopOut: Example Games”) shows both players playing rather well and
Red needing the repetition to salvage a draw.
Strategic Principles of PopOut
Same parity as CONNECT 4. In ordinary CONNECT 4, after Black takes turn
10, there will be ten disks on the board (five red, five black). The same applies in
PopOut ifthere have been no pop-out moves. IfBlack plays a pop-out move at his
or her turn 10, there will be eight disks on the board (five red, three black). Thus,
in either case there will be an even number ofdisks after Black’s move. Similarly,
there will always be an odd number of disks on the board after any Red move,
regardless of how many pop-out moves have been played. For this reason, the
fundamentalstrategic rule of CONNECT 4 still applies : Red (First Player) tries to
establish a winning odd-row threat; Black (Second Player) is more likely to win
with an even-row threat.
Nonwinning pop-out moves are usually wrong. As I just mentioned, if
Black plays a pop-out move at turn 10, he or she will be left with three black disks
to Red’s five red disks. This is a huge handicap, so pop-out moves must not be
228

made lightly. In most games of PopOut, play will proceed without any pop-out
moves at all until Red or Black plays a pop-out move and wins at once. There
are exceptions, however, and in the illustrative games I describe examples where a
player needs two pop-out moves to win.
Draws are rare. Because the pop-out move gives players new ways to win,
drawn games are uncommon in PopOut. This keeps the tension and excitement
high! When play is going poorly in ordinary CONNECT 4, a player can abandon
his or her own offense and try to secure a draw, but this tactic won’t work in
PopOut. Red must be especially alert for a possible Black pop-out move creating a
second-row win.
CONNECT
Play in lower rows. In ordinary 4, the third row is usually key,
and it is often correct to grab the third-row cell whenever a second-row play by the
opponent allows the possibility. Cells on the fourth and fifth rows are also often
CONNECT
valuable. Ifyou’ve mastered 4, you’ve learned to play on the fourth
or fifth row in many situations where a less-advanced player might think a first- or
second-row play is more logical. This principle changes in PopOut. Since pop-out
moves are possible only with first-row cells ofa player’s own color occupying the
,
first row becomes more desirable. Even when a player has no intention of ever
popping that disk out, the fact that the player occupies that first-row cell prevents
the opposing player from playing a pop-out move there. Furthermore, because of
thepossibilityofasuddenwin on thefirstorsecond rowafterapop-out move, disks
CONNECT
on the second row are more valuable in PopOut than in ordinary 4.
CONNECT
Red should win. In ordinary 4, Red must play almost perfectly
to win against an expert Black player. In PopOut, Red can relax somewhat: His or
her advantage is much stronger. The same move a player would make in ordinary
CONNECT
4 will often work in PopOut; the player must just remember to lean
toward a first- or second-row play in close situations where he or she would lean
CONNECT
toward a play higher in the board in ordinary 4. Black should try to
occupy cells in the first, second, and third rows and should look for a chance to
pop out a disk and win suddenly.
Ifyou and your partner get so good at PopOut that Red wins almost every
game, you may wish to handicap Red. Restrict Red to a single pop-out move per
game (letting Black still have unlimited pop-outs).
PopOut: Example Games
As far as I know, there has not yet been a complete computer analysis ofPopOut.
Red’s strongest first move is 1 Dl, but it probably is not the onlywinning move, so
it is not shown in these diagrams with an exclamation point.
229

would be a bad move, but the increased value offirst- and second-row cells makes
it fine here. In the diagram, Black may seem on the verge ofvictory, but Red pops
out at El and gets four-in-row. • •

and second-row moves are good in PopOut.) Unlike in ordinary 4,
Red’s disk 3 and disk 5 at D3 and D5 were not essential: First-row moves would
have worked also. For the same reason, Black’s 4 d4 was a weak move.

Sometimes a player will need two pop-out moves to win. Let’s see some
examples ofthat.
Thereweresomeweakmoves leadingup to Diagram 407, but from here Redwins
easily. First Red pops out at Dl (preventing the winning Black pop-out move at el).
Black might answer at e5, but Red then pops out at Gl andwins at D2-E2-F2-G2.
Diagram 408 is another example where Red may need two pop-out moves to
win. (IfRed were restricted to a single pop-out move, 7 Bl would not work: 7 E2
would be mandatory.)

235

Four-in-Row: Four disks ofthe same color in a continuous line horizontally, vertically, or
diagonally.
Future J Shape: A configuration ofRed disks, such as B1-C2-D3-D1-E2, with E3
empty. It is calledfutureJshape because Red can convert it to aJ shape whenever he or
she chooses.
Horizontal Threat: Cell whose occupation would form four-in-row along a horizontal
row.
J Shape: The common winning Red configuration comprising D1-D3-E2-E3 or its
mirror image.
Key Threat: A threat that is essential to victory.
Major Threat: An empty cell whose occupation would complete four-in-row, but which
is not available for immediate play.
Mate: Victor)" bycompletion offour-in-row.
Minor Threat: One oftwo (or three) empty cells in a partiallycompleted four-in-row.
Mixed Threat: One ofa pair ofminor threats, ofwhich one is odd and one is even; can
occur only along a diagonal.
Odd-Row Threat: A major or minor threat cell on the third or fifth row.
Odd-Threated Column: Anycolumn with an odd-row threat. Each odd-threated
column implies an eventual reversal ofzugzwang control. (A column with same-player
threats in both the second and fifth rows may be treated as two odd-threated columns.)
Opening: The initial move sequence ofa game, especially a standard, well-played
sequence.
Overcut J Shape: A Red J shape together with three Black disks above it that negate
Red’s triple threat.
Overcutting Threat: A threat that neutralizes an enemy threat immediately below it.
Normally the overcutting threat will be a major threat, and the threat under it will be a
minor threat.
Plethora ofThreats: A group offive or more threats that work together to have strong
strategic value.
Pop-Out Move: A move in which a player removes a disk instead ofadding one. This is
available onlywith Hasbro’s newer game sets.
Push-Up Play: A tactic in which a player occupies cells such as C1-C2-C3 to ensure his
or her capture ofC5.
236

Red: The player who moves first. (Any color may be used, but in this book I assume that
First Player-uses red disks.)
Row Parity: The attribute ofa threat determined bywhether it lies on an even-
numbered or odd-numbered row.
A
Sextuple Threat: particular configuration ofsix threats equivalent to a triple threat.
Strategic Threat: A cell that a player hopes eventually to occupy (or threaten to occupy)
as part ofa four-in-row but that is not an immediate check.
Tactical Sequence: A series oftwo or more forcing moves to attain an objective.
Tempo-Gaining Move: A playwhich forces the opponent to play an otherwise-useless
disk to block a threat. The player who makes a tempo-gaining move gets to play, in
effect, two good disks in a row.
Third-Row Gambit: An opening style in which Red succeeds in forming a third-row
threat on one side ofthe board, but Black compensates with fifth-row threats on the
opposite side.
Threat: A valuable empty cell. Threats are classified as Red or Black; strategic or tactical;
major or minor; even-row or odd-row; horizontal, diagonal, or vertical. (A single cell
may represent more than one threat.)
Transposition: Reaching the same position by a different ordering ofthe moves.
Triple Mixed Threat: Two (or more) minor odd-row threats in two separate columns,
which are made equivalent in value to a major odd-row threat by virtue ofone or more
even-row threats in one ofthe two columns.
Triple Odd-Row Threat: Three minor odd-row threats in three separate columns. This
is usually equivalent in value to a major odd-row threat.
Triple Threat: Either a triple mixed threat or a triple odd-row threat.
Undercutting Threat: A threat that neutralizes an opposing player’s threat above it.
Winning Quadruple Threat: A particular configuration offour threats equivalent to
either (1) two major odd-row threats or (2) an adjacent major threat pair.
Zugzwang: A position in which a player doesn’t want to move and would prefer to pass if
that were legal. Most CONNECT 4 games are won in the ending after the loser is placed
in zugzwang and forced to play. Zugzwang cannot occur in most games ofthe tic-tac-toe
CONNECT
family, but the “gravity rule” makes 4 an exception.
Zugzwang Control: The dominant (or upper-hand) position in zugzwang. Normally
Red has zugzwang control when there are an odd number ofodd-threated columns;
Blackhas it when there are an even number ofsuch columns.

Answers to Problem Set (Expert)
I
Problem 241: Black wins with 22 d6, eventually establishing a sixth-row
horizontal threat (related to Diagram 163).
Problem 242: Black wins with 18 d6, preventing Red diagonal threats at D6-G3
(see Diagram 51).
Problem 243: Red wins with 17 E2, followed byA2 after e3 to destroy Black's
second-row horizontal threat (see Diagram 322).
Problem 244: Red wins with 19 G2; Red has a plethora ofthreats, including the
B5-E2 diagonal and the E column; G2 is the way to activate the
right side tactically (see Diagram 309).
Problem 245: Black draws with 22 b6 (related to Diagram 361).
Problem 246: Black wins with 16 d6 (related to Diagram 331).
Problem 247: Red wins with 21 C2, forming the triple threat A3-A4-C5 (related
to Diagram 319).
Problem 248: Black draws with 20 fl, preventing a Red threat at C4 (see
Diagram 215).
Problem 249: Black wins with 10 b4, blocking Red’s A5-D2 diagonal; Red’s
B2-E5 diagonal is less urgent because Black can undercut it (see
Diagram 271).
Problem 250: Black wins with 16 e5, building a major threat at d4 (see Diagram
141).
Problem 251: Red draws with 29 F4, followed by B6 (F4 stops a quick Black win,
and also forces Black to spend his turn preventing Red’s winning
F5 follow-up, so Red gets B6 as well) (see Diagram 126).
Problem 252: Red wins with 15 D6, the D6-G3 diagonal forming part ofa
sextuple threat (related to Diagram 369).
Problem 253: Black wins with 14 d2, making even-row threats (see Diagram
289).
Problem 254: Red wins with 17 Cl, interfering with Black’s possible threat at e3
(related to Diagram 300).
252

Problem 255: Red wins with 17 A 4, to deny Black a fourth-row horizontal threat
(related to Diagram 226).
Problem 256: Red wins with 17 A2, a temporizing move better than B1 (related
to Diagram 367).
Problem 257: Red wins with 19 A1 (related to Diagram 55).
Problem 258: Red wins with 11 C4, building a threat at E2 (related to Diagram
112
).
Problem 259: Red wins with 21 El, aiming at threats on the upper left side
(Red’s threat at B3 is partly undercut by Black’s b2-e5 diagonal)
(related to Diagram 198).
Problem 260: Black draws with 16 b2, eliminating Red’s second-row tactical
threats (related to Diagram 299).
Problem 261: Red wins with 23 G5, depriving Black ofa potential threat at f5
(related to Diagram 325).
Problem 262: Red wins with 21 D6, preventing the Black triple threat f5-f4-g3
(related to Diagram 368).
Problem 263: Black wins with 24 a3, forcing the construction ofa major threat at
a5 or b5 (see Diagram 342).
Problem 264: Red wins with 19 D3, blocking some possible Black left-side
threats; victory will come at D5 eventually (related to Diagram
285).
Problem 265: Red wins with 19 A4, avoiding a future Black fourth-row threat
(related to Diagram 184).
Problem 266: Red wins with 21 G5, interfering with Black’s possible threats at
e5-f5 (related to Diagram 383).
Problem 267: Red wins with 21 Gl, racing to capture E6 eventually (see
Diagram 208).
Problem 268: Black draws with 18 b2, threatening push-up plays in both
columns B and D (related to Diagram 261).
Problem 269: Red wins with 21 Gl, the only safe move (related to Diagram 384).
Problem 270: Red draws with 23 C6; Red can’t win so just stops Black from
getting a winning sixth-row threat (related to Diagram 18).
253

Answers to Problem Set J (Challenger)
Problem 271: Red wins with 25 G2, later capturing G5 with a push-up play
(related to Diagram 347).
Problem 272: Black wins with 22 d6, the only way to get an even-row threat
(related to Diagram 69).
Problem 273: Red draws with 9 E2; this move loses forcing value if D2 is played
first (related to Diagram 82).
Problem 274: Red draws with 23 F6, blocking Black threats e5 and e6 (related to
Diagram 216).
Problem 275: Red wins with 11 FI, with B2 or C2 to follow for a left-side win
(related to Diagram 231).
Problem 276: Red wins with 21 A3, blocking Black’s third-row threat (see
Diagram 187).
Problem 277: Red wins with 17 D6; Red already has a triple threat, but d6 would
give Black a triple threat ofhis or her own (related to Diagram
272).
Problem 278: Red draws with 13 El, blocking Black’s b4 threat and preserving a
possible future Red counterthreat at B5 (related to Diagram 256).
Problem 279: Red wins with 15 E6, getting a triple threat after B3 (or after B4
and B5 or B6) (see Diagram 210).
Problem 280: Red wins with 29 A2, so that Black c4 can be answered at C5,
with a quick win to follow (related to Diagram 248).
Problem 281: Black draws with 22 f3, followed by f5 to block the fifth row
(related to Diagram 124).
Problem 282: Red wins with 19 A2, aiming at a B3 threat to undercut Black’s
threat at b4 (see Diagram 292).
Problem 283: Red wins with 13 G2, preventing a Black second-row threat (see
Diagram 288).
Problem 284: Black wins with 18 g2, after which either f3 or f4 gives Black a key
major threat (related to Diagram 339).
Problem 285: Red wins with 19 E2, destroying Black’s second-row threat (related
to Diagram 58).
Problem 286: Red draws with 19 A4 (see Diagram 361).
254

Problem 287: Red wins with 27 F6, since a Black major threat at e5 would
override Red’s minor threat there (related to Diagram 204).
Problem 288: Red wins with 23 A2; Red already has a triple threat (A3-A4-C5)
on the left side, but the tactical possibility ofdouble check after
Red A3 and C4 means that A2 gives Black no time to construct a
counterthreat (related to Diagrams 319-21).
Problem 289: Red wins with 23 G3; Black has strong tactical possibilities in
the upper right, which Red can destroy only with counterplay
involving a column G vertical threat (related to Diagram 341).
Problem 290: Red wins with 23 FI (related to Diagram 363).
Problem 291: Black draws with 24 d2; ifRed plays there, he or she gets a
winning, albeit unusual, triple threat (related to Diagram 176).
Problem 292: Red wins with 15 Al, interfering with Black’s potential threat
along the al-d4 diagonal. B2 would fail since it lets Black get b3,
which works well with either c3 or c4 (this position is from the
opening shown in Diagram 383).
Problem 293: Red wins with 15 D6; B4 is also a useful cell but would let Black
take b5 (related to Diagram 384).
Problem 294: Black wins with 24 a5, building the left-side triple threat b4-b5-c5;
by itselfthe triple threat would be enough only to draw, but Black
has other potential threats, for example, on the b3-e6 diagonal (see
Diagram 318).
Problem 295: Red wins with 17 F5, with the plethora ofthreats shown in
Diagram 390; B5 is less valuable because Red’s disk at FI renders it
redundant for the C4-E2 diagonal.
Problem 296: Red draws with 15 Al, blocking the d4 diagonal and aiming at
A1-A2-A3 (related to Diagram 157).
Problem 297: Red wins with 11 F3 to make minor third-row threats (see
discussion at Diagram 392).
Problem 298: Black wins with 18 a2, avoiding a Red undercutting threat along
the A2-D5 diagonal (see Diagram 160).
Problem 299: Red draws with 15 D2, to block the second row and aim at
diagonal threat A5-D2 (related to Diagram 140).
Problem 300: Red wins with 15 C2, destroying Black’s second-row horizontal
threat (see Diagram 55).
255

GAMES
CONQUER
Fun to play on a game board or computer, CONNECT 4
has been enthusiastically embraced by fans everywhere.
This definitive book will hone your CONNECT 4 skills like
no other, tracing how the game was invented, revealing the
strategic theories of a puzzle expert, and providing actual
examples for testing what you’ve learned.
Before long, you’ll be a lean, mean, disk-dropping machine,
ready for CONNECT 4 success!
JAMES DOW ALLEN is a computer scientist and inventor with
32 U.S. patents. In addition to professional work with device
firmware, operating systems, and compression algorithms,
Allen pursues programming as a hobby. In 1988 he developed
a computer solver for the game of CONNECT 4 just for fun.
He is semiretired, enjoying a quiet life with his wife and two
young children.
